市場疑慮拖累美股，短線聚焦 Fed 決策與財報
2026 年 9 月 15 日
一、 9/14 美股下挫、美債殖利率曲線熊市趨平，且油價大漲
1. 股票指數：
⚫ 道瓊工業指數下跌 0.29%，收 52,421.20 點；
⚫ S&P 500 指數下挫 0.48%，收 7,619.98 點；
⚫ 那斯達克綜合指數下跌 0.56%，收 26,186.41 點；
⚫ 費城半導體指數下跌 5.86%，收 11,131.28 點。
2. 當日重要個股表現：
⚫ 在科技類股方面，輝達下跌約 3.4%、美光下跌 5.3%、邁威爾下跌 7.3%；

⚫ 通訊類股方面，Netflix 上漲 3.8%、Take-Two 上漲 3.5%、Fox 上漲 4.6%；
⚫ 軟體類股方面 ，ServiceNow、Adobe、Workday 等軟體股則上漲約 4% 至
7.4%。
3. 美國債市漲跌不一、殖利率曲線走勢熊市趨平。2 年期美國公債殖利率上升 3.24 個基
點至 4.66%，10 年期殖利率上升 2.06 個基點至 4.99%，10 年期殖利率一度升破 5%，
而 30 年期殖利率則下跌約 0.76 個基點至 5.34%。短率上升，反映市場對未來美國聯
準會升息的預期。
4. 油價因中東衝突與供給風險再度升高而上漲 ，WTI 原油上漲 1.81%，收在每桶 101.86
美元，布蘭特原油則上漲 1.56%，收在 106.24 美元。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. AI 開發放緩疑慮，壓抑硬體需求預期：Anthropic 執行長 Dario Amodei 於上週末
呼籲放慢前沿 AI 能力提升速度，並提出第三方安全評估與業界協調方案，使 AI 擴

策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
張節奏成為新的不確定因素。我們研判，半導體位於 AI 建置支出的上游，股價對新
增算力投資的成長速度尤其敏感。即使既有訂單未立即改變，只要市場降低對未來數
季增速的期待，GPU、高頻寬記憶體及網通設備供應鏈的評價便可能先行調整；目前
仍須區分投資預期降溫與實際訂單下修。
2. 中東能源設施遇襲，油價推升通膨風險：沙烏地阿拉伯能源設施及中東船舶遇襲，引
發供應疑慮，布蘭特原油期貨 9 月 14 日結算價升至每桶 106.24 美元。油價持續偏
高可能透過運輸與生產成本以及民眾實質購買力影響企業獲利；若進一步傳導至通膨

預期，將延長貨幣政策維持緊縮的時間，對股市評價形成壓力。
3. 十年美債殖利率一度突破 5%，評價與融資承壓：美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中突破
5%，為 2023 年以來首見。殖利率上升提高未來獲利的折現率，對依賴長期成長預
期支撐評價的股票較不利。另一方面，資料中心若仰賴借款擴張，融資成本上升也會
提高投資報酬門檻，可能影響新增建置計畫，形成 AI 供應鏈面臨的另一項壓力。
4. 通膨月增回升 ， 升息前觀望氣氛加重 ：BLS 於 9 月 11 日公布 ，8 月 CPI 月增 0.4%
，高於 7 月的 0.1%，核心 CPI 月增率也由 0.2% 升至 0.3%。我們認為，短期物
價動能回升，加上能源價格偏高，提高聯準會維持緊縮政策的壓力。投資人除了評估
當次升息，也須重新衡量後續利率高點與維持時間，增加 FOMC 會議前高波動股票
的調整壓力。
5. 成長與評價雙重重估，放大半導體跌幅：本波股市回檔壓力同時來自獲利成長預期與
市場願給的評價倍數。AI 開發放緩疑慮影響未來營收想像，殖利率上行則壓低未來獲

利的現值。當股價已反映較高成長期待，只要新增訊息不如預期，即使當期營收仍強
勁，也可能出現明顯修正。後續應觀察獲利上修能否抵銷評價收縮，不能僅以跌幅大
就認定股價便宜。

不另行通知。
三、市場看法
1. 龍頭財報仍具成長，支持中期正向看法：輝達 8 月 26 日公布的 2027 會計年度第
二季資料中心營收達 890 億美元、年增 117%，並預估第三季總營收 1,080 億美元
、上下浮動 2%。博通 9 月 2 日公布的 2026 會計年度第三季 AI 半導體營收為
167 億美元、年增 221%，並預估第四季達 217 億美元。上述實績與公司展望，是
我們對 AI 核心供應鏈維持中期正向看法的依據；惟這些財測發布於本次事件之前，
仍須追蹤後續更新。
2. 放緩方案尚待落地，需求影響須分項評估：Anthropic 執行長 Amodei 明確表示，放

緩不代表停止模型訓練，但也提出研議訓練算力等限制，因此實際影響取決於後續執
行方式。既有模型的商業使用、推論與安全測試仍可能帶來算力需求，惟能否抵銷前
沿訓練投資的放緩，必須由使用量、訂單與收入驗證。此時適合保留成長觀點，同時
提高對支出變化的敏感度。
3. 科技股仍有輪動，軟體與半導體強弱分明：9/14 當日企業軟體與資安股逆勢上漲，顯
示科技股內部仍有輪動與受惠程度的差異。我們認為，若前沿 AI 開發放緩，部分既
有軟體業者面臨的競爭及產品替代壓力可能減輕；AI 安全疑慮升高，也可能強化資安
、權限控管及企業治理需求。相較之下，半導體更直接承受新增建置支出預期變化。
因此，9/14 當日費半重挫尚不足以推論整體科技需求同步轉弱；後續宜比較訂單能見
度、訂閱收入、獲利與現金流，單日相對強勢仍須由基本面延續性驗證。
4. 升息機率約九成，會後政策態度才是關鍵：依 Reuters 於美東 9 月 14 日收盤報導
引述的 CME FedWatch 資料，本次升息一碼的機率約 90%。若美東 9 月 16 日

FOMC 確實升息 0.25 個百分點，利率區間將升至 3.75% 至 4.00%，當次升息已
大致反映於市場預期。我們認為，會後聲明及主席記者會對未來利率政策的態度才是
關鍵：是否暗示還需升息、利率須維持高檔多久，以及是否擔心能源漲價向核心通膨

大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
擴散，並須搭配點陣圖判讀。若後續緊縮訊號未超出預期、長債殖利率趨穩，有利評
價修復；若利率路徑較預期更高、更持久，科技股仍可能承壓。
5. 通膨改善仍不均衡，避免過早判定轉折：8 月核心 CPI 年增率為 2.4%，低於 7 月
的 2.5%，但其月增率由 0.2% 升至 0.3%，顯示年增放緩與短期動能回升並存。後
續須觀察能源漲價是否向服務價格與通膨預期擴散。若油價回穩、核心物價再度降溫
，利率壓力可望緩和；若能源衝擊持續，股市調整時間可能拉長。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦 Fed 決策與 10 月美股財報，科技股中期向上趨勢料不致有變：我們目前仍

維持 AI 核心供應鏈中期正向看法，較看好能將營收成長轉化為獲利與現金流的企業
，但宜密切觀察本週的 FOMC 會議結果與 10 月中將起跑的美股財報。若雲端業者維
持資本支出、晶片公司財測穩定，且 FOMC 此次會議未釋出超預期緊縮訊號，有利
市場信心修復；然而，若出現廣泛支出下修、訂單遞延，或油價與殖利率持續上行，
則應拉長觀察期並重新檢視通膨與利率對於股市動能前景的影響。
2. 3~10 年美國公債/投資級債為債市首選：目前美國的通膨數據仍具韌性，且就業市場
穩健，使年底前市場升息預期再度升溫。市場對美國財政赤字、公債供給及長期通膨
仍要求較高風險補償，30 年期殖利率與短天期利差仍維持高檔，顯示期限溢酬尚未收
斂，考量當前 3～10 年期美國公債與投資級債能鎖定目前較高息收，且其受長端財政
風險影響也相對較小，故現階段3～10 年期美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對
較佳。

市場焦點
美國
• Fed褐皮書顯示，美國經濟整體維持溫和擴張，10個地區經濟活動小幅至溫和成長，消費及企
業需求仍有支撐 ，但住宅市場偏弱 、消費者價格敏感度升高 。就業僅小幅增加 ，薪資溫和成
長；另一方面 ，能源、運輸、原物料及關稅成本持續推升企業支出 ，物價在多數地區溫和上
升，反映經濟韌性與通膨壓力並存，增加Fed政策判斷難度。
美伊衝突
• 美國８月底宣布對逾 60個伊朗相關實體實施制裁 ，試圖切斷伊朗的石油 、金融與貿易收入 。
但伊朗歷經數十年制裁後 ，已建立相對成熟的制裁規避網絡 ，但短期預期美國仍難迫使德黑
蘭屈服。對金融市場而言 ，若制裁未進一步衝擊伊朗石油出口 ，油價上行空間相對有限；反
之，若美國擴大次級制裁並波及中國等主要買家，則可能推升原油價格與全球通膨預期。

韓股
• 儘管市場波動劇烈 ，韓國企業在第⼆季度財報季的盈利前景仍在持續改善 ，在三星電⼦和 SK
海力⼠公佈業績後，MSCI韓國指數的全年每股純益預期被小幅上調 ，此前5至6月修正步伐一
度放緩，如今⼜重新加快，整體來看，今年韓國盈利預期累計上調幅度接近 190%，並推動整
個新興市場盈利預期提升約 37%，晶⽚需求指引依然強勁 ，隨著盈利預測持續上調 ，台灣市
場也從中受益。

美國製造業維持擴張，需求降溫與高成本壓力牽制擴張動能
• 新訂單降溫，製造業維持擴張韌性 ：美國8月ISM製造業PMI降至54.6%，較7月55.6%回落，但仍連續第8個月位於擴張
區間；新訂單降至 53.7%、訂單積壓降至 51.8%，顯示需求動能較前月放緩 ，惟生產指數仍達 58.3%，連續第10個月擴
張，運輸設備、機械及石油與煤炭等主要產業維持成長，製造業整體仍具韌性。
• 生產韌性延續，價格壓力仍偏高：就業指數降至51.2%，雖較前月走弱但仍處擴張區間；供應商交貨指數升至59.3%，代
表供應商交期進一步拉長 ，而價格指數維持71.1%，原物料價格已連續 23個月上升，鋼鋁、關稅及石化產品成本仍是主
要壓力來源。整體呈現「需求降溫、生產偏強、成本仍高」的格局，企業利潤與通膨傳導仍需觀察。

• 後市觀察：製造業景氣仍具韌性 ，但新訂單放緩與投入成本高檔並存 ，後續應觀察需求能否延續 ，以及鋼鋁、能源與關
稅成本是否進一步轉嫁至終端價格上 。投資布局可聚焦半導體 、電機電⼦及AI基礎建設等受惠於製造投資與 AI資本支出
的領域；債券則以中短天期及投資等級公司債為主，降低利率波動風險，以股債配置分散景氣與利率風險。

褐皮書揭美國經濟溫和成長，成本壓力增添Fed政策難度
• 經濟溫和擴張，消費與企業需求仍有支撐：Fed褐皮書
顯示，自7月初至8月下旬，10個地區經濟活動小幅至
溫和成長、2個地區持平，消費支出整體小幅增加 ，製
造業、部分服務業及金融活動有所改善 ，資料中心與
國防相關需求亦提供支撐；但住宅市場表現仍偏弱 ，
消費者對價格更加敏感 ，顯示經濟維持韌性但內需表
現分化。
• 就業增幅有限 ，成本壓力仍牽動通膨 ：就業整體僅小
幅增加，7個地區就業成長 、5個地區大致持平 ，薪資
多數維持溫和上升；然而能源 、運輸、金屬、石化原
料及關稅成本仍持續推升企業投入成本 ，部分企業甚
至難以完全轉嫁成本，整體價格仍呈現上升。
• 市場觀察： 褐皮書反映美國經濟尚未明顯轉弱 ，但就

業降溫與通膨壓力並存，使Fed政策取向更具挑戰。近
期市場對9月升息的預期明顯升溫 ，政策仍高度依賴後
續通膨與就業數據 。建議債券配置宜以短中天期美債
及投資級公司債為主 ，降低長天期利率波動；股票則
聚焦大型優質科技 、AI基礎建設及高現金流企業 ，並
搭配黃金分散通膨與地緣政治風險。

制裁使伊朗經濟受創，仍難迫使政權屈服
上海合作組織(SCO)佔全球經濟(GDP)的四分之一
資料來源：IMF，Bloomberg，2026.9.1，其他上海合作組織成員國包括巴基斯坦、伊朗、哈薩克、烏茲別克、
白俄羅斯、蒙古、吉爾吉斯、塔吉克和阿富汗資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.8.26
伊朗絕大部分石油銷往中國
• 制裁聲勢強，但關鍵仍在中國：美國８月底宣布對逾60個伊朗相關實體實施制裁，試圖切斷伊朗的石油、金融與貿易收入。
制裁能否真正奏效，關鍵仍在於是否將中國納入主要打擊對象。伊朗近八成的石油銷往中國，美方現階段僅將少數中港企業
列入黑名單，刻意避開了中國大型國有金融與能源巨頭，若強行實施全面制裁，可能危及關鍵礦產供應、破壞脆弱的美中貿
易休兵，甚至推升全球能源價格。

• 制裁使伊朗經濟受創，仍難迫使政權屈服：伊朗歷經數十年制裁後，已建立相對成熟的制裁規避網絡，並透過中國、俄羅斯、
印度及中亞國家參與的上海合作組織（SCO）等非西方平台維持經貿往來。俄羅斯與伊朗合作持續深化，巴基斯坦亦表態無
義務遵守美方制裁，印度則在制裁壓力下維持一定程度的經貿交流。因此，美國新一輪制裁雖將加劇伊朗經濟壓力，但短期
仍難迫使德黑蘭屈服。對金融市場而言，若制裁未進一步衝擊伊朗石油出口，油價上行空間相對有限；反之，若美國擴大次
級制裁並波及中國等主要買家，則可能推升原油價格與全球通膨預期，進而延後聯準會降息時程，增加股債市場波動。

歐元區通膨率升至近三年新高，９月升息機率高
• 歐洲央行9月升息機率高：歐洲央行（ECB）9月升息至2.50%的預期已逐步升溫，主因歐元區8月通膨升至3.3%，加上能源
價格受伊朗戰事推升，強化鷹派對進一步緊縮的主張。不過，核心通膨降至2.4%、服務通膨趨緩，且長期通膨預期仍大致穩
定，顯示能源價格尚未明顯向薪資與核心物價擴散，因此，9月升息後暫停仍是較可能的政策路徑。 9月升息後，ECB是否於
12月再次升息，將取決於能源衝擊的持續時間及其向核心通膨的傳導程度。
• 12月是否再升息，能源價格是關鍵：若伊朗戰事延續、油氣價格維持高檔，鷹派可能推動利率於12月進一步升至2.75%；反
之，若能源價格回落、服務通膨持續降溫，則本輪升息可能在9月告終。整體而言，ECB政策已轉向偏鷹，但在缺乏能源衝

擊廣泛傳導至核心通膨的明確證據下，年內連續升息空間有限。在政策利率逐步觸頂的情況下，歐債殖利率若因升息預期而
上行，反而可提供較佳的中長期配置與收益機會。
歐元區通膨（CPI） ECBspeak指數暗示歐洲央行還會進一步加息

SK海力⼠公佈業績後，MSCI韓國指數的全年每股純益預期被小幅上調 ，此前5至6月修正步伐一度放緩，如今⼜重新加快，整

望成為第三季韓股上漲的重要動能，尤其是記憶體產業龍頭企業，擁有充沛的自由現金流，實施庫藏股買回，不僅能提升每股
價值，也反映管理層對未來獲利前景具信心，並認為目前股價被低估，參考日本推動公司治理改革後帶動股市重評價的經驗 ，

預期韓股後市仍具支撐力，若市場出現短期回檔，反而可視為逢低布局的機會。

期戰略方向，相關受惠企業可望持續成長 ，另一方面科網龍頭的AI業務已逐步走出高投入階段，開始展現商業化成果與獲利
潛力，投資回報能見度提升，在政策支持、技術自主及商業化加速三大因素帶動下 ，AI概念股若因短期波動出現回檔 ，仍可
視為中長期布局機會。

美債市場結構性轉變：從不挑剔的機構到追求回報的私人投資者
• 自新冠疫情以來 ，美國10年期公債殖利率持續走高 ，過去市場多半歸咎於聯準會升息或通膨 ，但彭博最新研究顯示 ，期限
溢價的顯著上升才是主要推手，其背後的根本原因是美債買家發生了結構性轉變。
• 傳統非價格敏感型買家縮手：過去幾十年，聯準會(透過QE)與外國央行是美債的兩大底氣來源，他們買美債是為了儲備與流
動性，對價格不敏感，因此能有效壓低利率與期限溢價 。自2020年以來，聯準會與外國官方持有的美債佔美國 GDP比重分
別下降12%與8%，光是這兩大買家的退場，就推升10年期美債期限溢價約1.05%。
• 私人投資者接棒，市場對殖利率極度挑剔： 目前的邊際買家已轉為外國私人投資者 (如基金、保險公司等)，私人買家會精算

美債、公司債、股票與各國主權債的收益差異，進行嚴格比價。當美債發行量增加時，必須給予更高的殖利率才能吸引他們
購買，這使得美債利率波動更加劇烈。
自疫情以來，長期利率呈上升趨勢
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.1

供給端新挑戰：AI浪潮帶動公司債暴增，搶奪資金池
• 科技巨頭發債融資潮 ，公司債成為美債的強勁替代品： 受人工智慧建設資本支出帶動 ，大型科技企業 (如Alphabet、Meta、
Amazon等)積極發行投資級公司債 。這些高品質科技公司債提供優異回報 ，吸引了大量私人資金從美債轉向公司債 。為了
與這些高收益的高評級公司債競爭資金，美國國債殖利率面臨更大的上行壓力。
• 小結及建議：當前美債殖利率居高不下，核心原因在於穩定的買家減少，而接手的私人買家變得挑剔，加上AI熱潮創造了高
回報的公司債競爭源。在美國財政部發債壓力未減的情況下，長端殖利率短期內難大幅下行。債券策略建議採取配置中短天
期債券，長天期公債(10年期以上)價格受期限溢價與供給壓力壓制，波動較大，建議縮短持債年期(如布局3-5年期)，鎖定高

殖利率的同時降低價格波動風險 ，加碼高品質投資級公司債，目前私人資金偏好高評級公司債(尤其是科技與AI龍頭債)，這
類債券信用風險極低，收益率卻優於同天期公債，是當前固定收益資產的優質選擇。
外國私人投資者購買美國公債和公司債
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.1
人工智慧相關債務發行只是殖利率上升的原因之一

日圓再現匯率干預跡象 套利交易平倉恐加劇日圓升值
• 日圓再現匯率干預跡象：上週週間，日圓兌美元匯價出現單日大幅升值情況，市場再度懷疑官方出手干預匯率。然而，彭博
指出，日本央行帳戶數據沒有大規模買入日元跡象，意味著匯率的劇烈波動可能來自市場重新調整日本央行加息預期，而非
日本政府再次入市干預。與此同時，美國財政部並未回應是否參與干預。
• 市場懷疑干預之際，套利交易平倉可能正在發生：儘管無法證實官方上週是否直接干預日圓匯價，但日圓走升可能也反映上
週日本央行官員高田創釋出鷹派升息的態度，將不排除一次性大幅升息或連續升息的可能性。根據芝加哥商品交易所的數據
顯示，日圓融資的套利交易正在平倉，而且中期內持有日圓而非其他G10貨幣的意願非常強烈。
• 觀點：2024年8月日本央行意外升息，引發日圓套利交易平倉，全球主要國家股市短期間出現修正，當時AI與半導體成為套

利資金撤離重點。本次匯價波動暫時可歸咎於套利交易倉位的調整，尚未出現恐慌賣壓，重點將關注在本月中的日本央行利
率會議，留意意外大幅升息的風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，截至2026.9.3。

• 今年年初黃金價格創下歷史新高，突破每盎司5,000美元大關，成為金融市場最受關注的資產之一，隨後經歷幾個月的高檔
修正後，上個月再度獲得市場資金追捧，現貨黃金價格單月大漲近10%。過去市場認為黃金上漲主要來自地緣政治風險與央
行降息預期，但近期投資邏輯似乎正在改變。
• 黃金多頭始自央行「去美元化」：本輪黃金多頭始自2022年烏俄衝突，西方國家凍結俄羅斯央行的大部分海外美元與歐元
外匯儲備，讓各國央行開始意識到外匯儲備的安全性，進而推動外匯儲備多元化。透過適度減持美元(美債)來佈局實體黃金
或其他國家貨幣，被成為「去美元化」趨勢。
• 「貶值交易」有望接棒黃金上漲動能：上個月美國公布最新國債規模已突破40兆美元，市場開始擔憂財政赤字的可維持性，
貨幣信心下降導致「貶值交易」發生(美債信心下降)，除了導致長天期美債殖利率上揚之外，貨幣避險需求再度讓黃金資產

成為市場焦點，8月份現貨黃金一度上漲超過15%，隨後雖因聯準會釋出鷹派訊號，單月漲幅仍接近10%。

• 央行買盤仍為黃金中期表現帶來利多：根據世界黃金協會2026年各國央行調查，上半年截至5/19，有45%的央行預期未來
一年將增加自身黃金儲備，創調查9年來新高。央行增持黃金主要考量包含：儲備多元化、美元走弱預期、地緣政治衝突以
及金價長期上漲趨勢，顯示黃金已成為各國儲備資產的重要戰略配置，在強勁的需求買盤下，多數機構預測黃金將維持多頭
至明年年底。
• 觀點：近期黃金價格反彈後，可能會受到獲利了結以及利率政策預期變化而出現波動，但在全球債務問題、貨幣貶值風險及
央行買盤支撐下，在中期內仍有機會走出慢牛行情。建議投資人以資產配置的角度，逢修正適度配置黃金資產(如：黃金ETF)。
風險接受度較高的投資人，則可關注金礦類股，但需注意其對於美元匯、利率的敏感度較高，資產價格波動較大。

前應詳閱基金公開說明書。

資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。

WinWay Technology
Valuation
We value WinWay using a PE-based approach that factors in earnings momentum and likely expansion of its addressable
market. Our target price of NT$11,500 is derived from applying c.41x to 2H27E-1H28E EPS, higher than the upper end of its
long-term PE range, which we believe is justified given the company's entrenched position in the high-end AI GPU testing
supply chain, rising contribution from coaxial socket and SLT solutions, and the increasing value of test content per module.

With these structural drivers in place, and as we believe the market is starting to value it as an AI/HPC play (rather than a
consumer electronics stock), we expect the multiple to hold at a premium relative to its historical averages.
Risks
Our quant rating system rates the WinWay stock High Risk based on its historical volatility.
Key downside risks that could impede the stock from reaching our target price and our earnings forecasts include: 1) client and

sector concentration – specifically any delays in US AI GPU product roadmap could impact the business outlook for WinWay; 2)
intensifying competition in coaxial and hypersockets from global peers; 3) faster-than-expected testing algorithm
optimization, which could shorten testing durations and reduce the anticipated hardware demand multiplier; and 4) delay of
MEMS Phase 2 mass production.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds

## Document 110: 9月通胀数据点评：上游价格延续强势，低位行业边际修复

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月通胀数据点评：上游价格延续强势，低位行业边际修复.pdf
Report source: 联储证券
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

证券研究报告 
宏观经济点评 2026 年 10 月 16 日

请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明

9 月通胀数据点评：上游价格延续强势，低位行业
边际修复

[Table_Author] 
 魏争 分析师  沈夏宜 分析师

Email:weizheng@lczq.com Email:shenxiayi@lczq.com  
证书:S1320524100001 证书:S1320523020004

### Page 2

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 2 
价格分别上涨 1.6%和 1.5%，五个行业合计拉动 PPI 环比约 0.63 个百分
点。二是煤炭价格延续高增。煤炭开采和洗选业价格环比上涨 9.5%，同
比由 26.6%升至 35.3%，我们分析煤炭价格高增或受供暖备煤需求增加、
供应阶段性偏紧及国际煤价预期影响。三是，AI 和优势制造业价格韧性仍
在。计算机通信和其他电子设备制造业价格同比由 5.3%升至 5.8%，电气
机械和器材制造业由 5.9%升至 6.2%，两类行业环比均上涨 0.4%，指向
算力、电子设备等高景气领域仍在提供结构性支撑。 
PPI 持续低迷行业并未进一步下探，部分领域出现 修复，但改善仍不广
泛。9 月，生活资料 PPI 同比下降 0.1%，降幅收窄 0.4 个百分点。其
中，食品类由下降 2.3%收窄至下降 1.8%，一般日用品由下降 0.8%收窄
至下降 0.6%，耐用消费品同比涨幅由 1.2%进一步提高至 1.6%。行业上
看，部分低位行业出现一定程度修复。一是建筑链条上游相关行业有改善
迹象。非金属矿物制品业价格在连续 7 个月下降后转为上涨 0.3%，同比
由-3.2%收窄至-2.5%，黑色金属冶炼和压延加工业环比上涨 0.2%，指向
钢材、水泥等需求或出现一定回升。二是下游部分行业出现降幅收窄。农
副食品加工业同比由-1.7%收窄至-0.9%；汽车制造业由-2.2%收窄至-
2.0%，医药制造业由-3.7%收窄至-3.3%，纺织服装由-1.2%收窄至-
1.0%。 
往后看，四季度政策发力有望为部 分低迷行业提供增量支撑，但价格结构
性特征大概率仍将延续。一方面，能源、有色和煤炭价格仍处较高水平，
叠加 AI 产业链需求和存储价格支撑，上游及优势制造业价格短期仍具有
较强韧性；另一方面，四季度，财政部安排使用 5500 亿元地方政府债务
结存限额，其中 2500 亿元专项债务结存限额定向支持四季度重大项目建
设，新建项目重点聚焦“六张网”等领域，随着项目落地并逐步形成实物
工作量，基建、城市更新等相关需求有望改善，或对当前仍处低位的黑
色、建材及部分中游制造业价格形成支撑，9 月建筑链价格环比转正也提
供了初步积极信号。但从当前生产资料与生活资料、核心 CPI 与总体 CPI
的分化来看，价格修复仍具有较强结构性，政策能否进一步推动价格由 局
部改善向广谱修复转变，关键仍在于财政发力形成实物工作量后，能否有
效带动企业投资、居民消费等其他内生需求同步改善。后续需重点关注黑
色、建材等低迷行业价格能否持续修复，以及生活资料 PPI、核心 CPI 和
服务价格能否接续改善。

风险提示：国际油价大幅波动超预期、国内需求修复不及预期。

投资评级说明 
投资建议的评级标准  评级 说明 
评级标准为报告发布日后的 6 个月内公司股价 （或行业
指数） 相对同期基准指数的相对市场表现。 其中A 股市
场以沪深 300 指数为基准； 新三板市场以三板成指 （针
对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针对做市转让标的）
为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准。 
股票评级 
买入 相对同期基准指数涨幅在 10%以上 
增持 相对同期基准指数涨幅在 5%~10%之间 
中性 相对同期基准指数涨幅在-5%~+5%之间 
减持 相对同期基准指数跌幅在 5%以上 
行业评级 
看好 相对表现优于市场 
中性 相对表现与市场持平 
看淡 相对表现弱于市场

## Document 121: [000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다실적에 수급을 더한다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다실적에 수급을 더한다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 5

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
여전히 가격 상승 구간 
SK하이닉스의 26년 3분기 매출액은 95.8조원, 영업이익 75.1조원으로 예상한다. 
이전 전망 대비 감소한 수준이다. 원/달러 환율 영향이다. 영업이익률은 78.4%로 전 
분기 대비 2.1%p 상승할 전망이다. DRAM 매출액은 73.1조원, 영업이익 
59.2조원으로 추정한다. DRAM ASP는 이전 전망 대비 상향 조정했다. 일부 제품 
가격 인상을 반영한 영향이다. Bit Growth는 이전 전망 수준을 유지했다. ASP 
상향이 수익성 개선에 미친 영향은 크지 않을 전망이다. NAND 매출액은 22.4조원, 
영업이익은 15.9조원으로 예상한다. 
Global Top 수준 주주환원 규모/자사주 살 게 더 있다 
SK하이닉스는 2026년 8월 19일 주주환원에 대한 새로운 정책을 제시했다. FCF에서 
CAPEX를 제외한 금액의 50% 이상을 주주환원에 사용한다는게 핵심이다. 2026년 
기준으로 최소 90조원 수준일 것으로 추정한다. 자사주 중심으로 주주환원을 계획 
중이고 이미 집행 중인 40조원 이외에 4분기 중에 추가로 자사주 매입이 진행될 
것으로 예상한다. 배당도 일부 집행이 되겠지만 자사주가 중심이 될 전망이다. 
흔들리지 말아야 할 이유는 많다 
SK하이닉스에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. AI 모델의 성장으로 메모리에 
대한 수요가 지속적으로 높아지고 있고, 이에 비해 공급 증가는 제한적인 상황이 
상당기간 지속될 것으로 전망하기 때문이다. 이로 인해 현재 수준의 DRAM, NAND 
가격에 변동성은 낮고, 높은 수익성이 유지되면서 안정적 현금흐름 창출이 가능할 
것으로 전망한다. 현재 주가는 지나치게 저평가되어 있는 국면에 있다고 판단한다. 
목표주가는 12개월 예상 EPS 446,092원에 PER 9 배를 적용한 4,000,000원을 
유지한다.

## Document 145: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies  _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.
EDITORIAL
RESILIENCE TO SHOCK AFTER SHOCK

### Page 2

4
EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank
for a changing
world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.
Article completed on 17 September 2026
Stéphane Colliac
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

## Document 170: Anthropic信用评级曝光：BB+，最高级别的垃圾级信用

Source PDF: all report pdf/Anthropic信用评级曝光：BB+，最高级别的垃圾级信用.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Anthropic ⾸获信⽤评级 BB+ ，最⾼级别的投机级（垃圾级）评分，仅⽐投资级低⼀档。评
级机构 KBRA 在信披极为有限的情况下，以 " ⻛险评分 " 替代完整财务模型完成评估。但
KBRA 相较于穆迪、标普和惠誉体量更⼩，⽽后三者将最终决定 Anthropic 上市后的正式信
⽤评级。
摘要：
AI 独⻆兽 Anthropic 的信⽤评级⾸次浮出⽔⾯。
据英国《⾦融时报》获得的⽂件显⽰，信⽤评级机构 Kroll Bond Rating Agency （ KBRA ）于今
年 6 ⽉授予 Anthropic BB+ 发⾏⼈评级 —— 即最⾼级别的投机级（垃圾级）评分，仅⽐投资级低
⼀档。该评级在 Anthropic 完成⼀笔由 Apollo 主导、规模达 350 亿美元的芯⽚融资交易之前获得，
此前从未公开披露。
这⼀评级对 Anthropic 的融资前景具有直接影响。华尔街各⼤银⾏正在筹备⼀笔 600 亿美元的新债
务⽅案，以帮助 Anthropic 收购更多由⾕歌和博通联合开发的定制 AI 芯⽚，其中包括 180 亿美元的

次级债务。能否跨越投资级⻔槛，将决定 Anthropic 能否以更低成本接触寿险公司、养⽼基⾦等
更⼴泛的机构投资者群体。
评级细节：有限信披下的 " ⻛险评分 "
KBRA 授予的 BB+ 评级附有稳定展望，针对的是 350 亿美元融资包中约 44 亿美元的次级债务 ——
这部分债务将投资者直接暴露于 Anthropic 的信⽤⻛险之下；其余部分则由芯⽚制造商博通提供
担保。该笔次级债以约 8.5% 的收益率发⾏，⾼级债的利率则为 5.75% 。
这⼀评级是在信息披露相当有限的情况下作出的。
据⽂件显⽰， KBRA 获得的财务信息包括营收、调整后营业收⼊以及 2026 年第⼀季度年化运营
收⼊，以及 Anthropic 提供的第⼆季度业绩内部预测，但⾃由现⾦流及 EBITDA 等关键财务指标均
未纳⼊披露范围。评级分析师不得不依赖假设⾃⾏估算，并最终采⽤ " ⻛险评分 " ⽅式替代传统的
完整财务模型来评估其信⽤状况。
财务表现：增速放缓，但⾼于预期

## Document 182: Business Checkpoint_ Looking into the logistics Profitability growth was positive in the third quarter, but logistics costs and supply chain pressures are creating challenges. 2 mi

Source PDF: all report pdf/Business Checkpoint_ Looking into the logistics Profitability growth was positive in the third quarter, but logistics costs and supply chain pressures are creating challenges. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13028181     1

Economy 
Business Checkpoint: Looking into the logistics 
15 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Small and mid-sized businesses entered Q3 in relatively solid financial condition, with profitability growth up across both 
groups, according to Bank of America business account data. Nearly half of estimated tariff payments had been refunded by 
September, though the share of these refunds relative to revenue has peaked. 
• In Bank of America business payments data, logistics spending growth per client was up four times as much for small than mid-
sized firms. Additionally, distribution and inventory transactions growth per client has outpaced spending since the spring of 
2025. 
• Growing transportation bottlenecks are re-emerging ahead of the holiday season. Trucking capacity has tightened as driver 
availability remains constrained, according to BofA Global Research. This has pushed freight rates higher and driven strong 
transportation sector payroll growth, particularly among mid-sized businesses, according to Bank of America payments data.

Business Checkpoint is a new, regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It provides a real-time view of the financial health, opportunities 
and challenges facing small and mid-sized businesses across the US economy, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America's proprietary 
data. By analyzing activity among both small and mid-sized firms, Business Checkpoint offers timely insights into business profitability, hiring, 
investment and spending trends. Such data is not intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, 
financial condition or performance of Bank of America. 
Businesses end Q3 in fair shape 
While profitability growth remained positive for both small and mid-sized firms in the third quarter, small firms outpaced their 
mid-sized counterparts for the second consecutive quarter (Exhibit 2). Still, there’s been a notable reversal in the profitability of 
mid-sized businesses, growing this quarter after declining slightly for the past two.  
The continued rollout of tariff refunds is serving as a tailwind to some firms' cash positions. That is particularly true for small 
firms. According to Bank of America account data, by September approximately 47% of estimated International Emergency 
Economic Powers Act-related tariff payments had been refunded to companies with less than $50 million in annual revenue.

According to Bank of America business payments data, the percentage point difference in tariff refunds as a share of revenue 
for firms with less than $50 million in annual revenue spiked in June but dropped to nearly zero in September. At the same time, 
the percentage point difference in tariff payments as a share of expenses began rising again relative to the 2023 average 
(Exhibit 1). This suggests that while tariffs continue to affect companies, the refund program has provided temporary relief for 
some businesses.  
Wholesale trade along with transportation and warehousing posted solid profitability growth across business size 
However, the benefits appear uneven. Such refunds have boosted cash flow, but they have not fully insulated firms from broader 
cost pressures.  
Looking across industries, mid-sized construction and wholesale firms had the strongest profitability growth in September 
(Exhibit 3). Notably, transportation and warehousing, as well as wholesale trade were among the few sectors showing positive 
profitability growth for both small and mid-sized firms last month. 
The persistence of logistics, freight and inventory-related cost pressures may help explain why profitability gains have remained 
relatively concentrated in transportation and wholesale trade. However, these sectors also have a higher exposure to rising gas 
prices within their margins (read more on this in the August Small Business Checkpoint). 
Exhibit 3: For both small and mid-sized businesses, profitability growth was positive in transportation and warehousing as well as wholesale trade 
in September 
Profitability growth by sector and business size in September (YoY%, six-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The cost of maintaining supply chains is rising 
In fact, the prices paid component of the September services reading from the Institute for Supply Management indicates still-
building price pressure for firms (Exhibit 4). This marked the highest reading since July 2022.   
Using Bank of America internal data, we identified payments to logistics and freight companies to assess a key part of supply 
chain costs. In June, logistics and freight payments’ growth per client from small businesses grew nearly 30%, though has 
moderated to 12.7% YoY in September (Exhibit 5). Meanwhile, mid-sized firms’ payments growth in this category was up just 
3.2% YoY in September. 
The divergence suggests logistics costs may be disproportionately affecting smaller firms, who are typically less capable of 
absorbing rising costs than larger firms. Larger companies also often benefit from dedicated logistics contracts and greater 
purchasing power, while smaller ones may be more exposed to spot-market transportation costs and fluctuations in freight 
pricing.

Evidence of more moving parts in companies’ supply chains 
Yet there is increasing evidence that tariffs and geopolitics are adding complexity to small and mid-sized companies’ supply 
chains. 
In Bank of America payments data, average distribution and inventory transaction growth per business client has increased 
steadily since the spring of 2025 despite spending growth in this category falling in September (Exhibit 6). Rising transaction 
growth alongside softer spending growth suggests businesses may be engaging with more suppliers, shipments or inventory 
movements even as overall procurement outlays moderates.  
Furthermore, in May 2026, the Global Supply Chain Pressure Index reached its highest reading since the summer of 2022 and has 
continued to remain elevated into October (Exhibit 7). This further emphasizes that transportation bottlenecks, delays and 
procurement challenges are becoming more common globally. While current readings remain below the extreme levels observed 
during the pandemic, the recent move higher indicates supply chain normalization may be reversing.

The trials of trucking  
As of October 1, BofA Global Research's proprietary bi-weekly Truckload Demand Indicator (see Methodology) remained below 
its historical growth signal (Exhibit 9) and the Truck Capacity Indicator, which reflects shippers' views of available capacity, 
tightened significantly, falling from 39.2 to 28.4 (Exhibit 8). This suggests shippers are seeing fewer trucks available relative to 
demand than in the previous survey. 
According to BofA Global Research, the current trucking upcycle is being driven by constrained supply rather than a significant 
improvement in freight demand. While any positive movement in demand is an important indicator as the industry moves into 
peak shipping season between late September and early December, tighter capacity appears to be the dominant force 
supporting freight rates. 
Exhibit 8: Shippers’ view of available capacity has fallen 
significantly as of October 1, 2026

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The reduction in capacity reflects several factors, including stricter commercial driver's license (CDL) and training requirements, 
increased compliance and enforcement measures, and other regulatory changes that have reduced the pool of qualified drivers. 
This has also likely driven some wage inflation within the industry. According to Bank of America payments data, transportation 
and warehousing payroll growth per client has surged since the start of the year, especially for mid-sized businesses (Exhibit 10).  
Having to pay higher wages suggests labor shortages remain a challenge in the sector. Firms may increasingly need to offer 
higher compensation to attract and retain workers as trucking capacity tightens. In turn, such capacity puts upward pressure on 
freight rates, particularly in the spot market where small businesses may be more impacted.

Logistics payments tend to increase in the second half of the year 
September marks a key run-up to the holiday season (read more on signs of holiday spending in the October Consumer 
Checkpoint), with spending on ocean shipping, containers, and other transportation services steadily accelerating ahead of the 
peak shopping period.  
In fact, over the past two years, the month of November has seen ocean shipping/container spending volumes well above their 
2023-2025 monthly average (Exhibit 11). The seasonal pattern likely reflects inventory restocking and holiday-related shipping 
activity. Ocean shipping volumes typically accelerate during the fall months as businesses prepare for year-end demand. 
Exhibit 11: Ocean shipping/container spending volumes were well above their 2023-2025 monthly average in November

Distributors and inventory payments’ growth fell ahead of holiday season 
Changes in logistics and freight spending often provide an early signal of inventory trends, as distributors account for a large 
share of supply chain-related spending (Exhibit 12). 
The sharp moderation in distribution and inventory spending growth per client for both small and mid-sized businesses indicates 
that much of the large-scale inventory accumulation seen earlier in 2025 has already occurred (Exhibit 13). In other words, 
businesses appear to be replenishing inventories for seasonal demand rather than significantly expanding stockpiles as they did 
earlier in the year. 
Overall, businesses seem to be entering the holiday season in relatively stable financial condition, supported by tariff refunds 
and positive profitability growth. However, rising logistics costs, tighter trucking capacity and elevated supply chain pressures 
suggest operating conditions are becoming more challenging.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business and/or Business Banking clients with a deposit account or a 
Small Business credit card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of 
America per Business client data represents activity spending from active Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card and at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and are 
generally defined as under $5mm in annual sales revenue. Mid-sized businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America 
and are generally defined as $5mm-$50mm in annual sales revenue. 
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Revenue tiers are determined by the combination of following factors: 1) stated revenue on small businesses credit applications, 2) actual 
account inflow into Bank of America Deposit Accounts, and 3) third party revenue estimation.  
The alternative hiring indicator consists of payments from Bank of America business clients to business-focused hiring firms which include both 
direct deposits through Automated Clearing House (ACH) and payments via credit and debit cards. 
Supply chain-related spending is comprised of payments to logistics, freight, distribution, inventory, delivery service, and trucking companies. 
These are identified from merchant names and MCC codes. 
BofA Global Research’s trucking indicators are a bi-weekly diffusion-style survey of shipper demand expectations over the next 0–3 months, 
built from responses from surveyed shippers. 
Data regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 189: Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-08).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-09-08
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Commodities: From Inflation Hedge to Structural Growth Asset
Anna Snider, Managing Director and Head of Investment Manager Selection
(Due Diligence)
A broader role in portfolios: For decades, commodities occupied a relatively narrow
role in portfolios: protection against inflation when rising prices eroded the value
of traditional assets. That function remains important, but the investment case is

broadening. Commodities now sit at the intersection of geopolitical fragmentation,
supply-chain security, reshoring, inventory rebuilding, and the buildout of AI and digital
infrastructure. Together, these forces are turning parts of the asset class from purely
cyclical exposures into potential long-term growth beneficiaries.
The diversification case is also becoming more relevant. In Q1, when inflation concerns
peaked, the Bloomberg Commodity Index returned close to 25%, outperforming both

Equities and Fixed Income. At the same time, the relationship among major asset
classes has shifted. Equity and Fixed Income correlations have tightened in recent years,
while commodities have moved in the opposite direction: over the trailing five years,
their correlation with both Equities and bonds has been negative. That combination of
inflation sensitivity and differentiated return behavior can make commodities useful
beyond a simple tactical hedge.

AI infrastructure is a physical buildout: AI is often viewed through semiconductors,
software platforms, and hyperscale data centers. Yet the infrastructure required to
deploy AI is extraordinarily commodity intensive. It requires continuous electricity,
generation capacity, transmission lines, substations, transformers, cooling systems,
networking hardware and structural materials. In that sense, AI is not only a computing
revolution. It is also an energy, infrastructure, and critical-materials investment cycle.

Power is the first constraint. Data centers require reliable, around-the-clock electricity.
Natural gas remains a practical source of incremental generation in the U.S., while
nuclear power is emerging as a strategic long-term option for hyperscalers seeking
stable baseload supply. Solar and wind can add capacity and support decarbonization
goals, but grid reliability and storage become more important as power demand rises.
That supports investment in batteries, backup power and grid-scale storage, with lithium

and graphite among the relevant inputs.
The buildout also increases demand for industrial metals. Copper is a clear beneficiary
because it is essential to transmission, transformers, cooling systems and data-center
wiring. Aluminum and iron ore are used in facilities, electrical equipment, transmission
networks and supporting infrastructure. Semiconductor fabrication adds another layer
of demand through industrial gases such as helium and neon, high-purity quartz for

wafers and optics, and gallium and germanium for advanced chips, photonics, and
telecommunications. AI-enabled automation and robotics also depend on permanent
magnets that use rare earth elements, including neodymium-praseodymium and
dysprosium-terbium.
Demand has moved faster than supply: This demand surge resembles earlier cycles
in semiconductors and memory, where expectations pulled spending forward. Metals
and mining companies are already seeing earnings growth and margin expansion,

yet the industry has struggled to keep pace with demand. The imbalance matters
because commodity supply is slow to respond. Production of many strategic materials
is geographically concentrated, permitting timelines are lengthy, recycling capacity is
limited, and substitution can remain years from commercial viability. New mines and
processing facilities can take a decade or more to reach production.
Investment Implications
Commodities may increasingly serve

three portfolio purposes: inflation
protection, diversification from
traditional assets, and participation in
long-term infrastructure and resource-
demand growth, supporting a diversified
allocation across direct commodities
and select producer Equities rather than
a single directional trade.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Governments are also taking a larger role through industrial policy, export controls,
stockpiling and efforts to secure critical supply chains. Restrictions involving gallium,
germanium, graphite and other strategic materials underscore how geopolitics can affect
availability and pricing. As a result, the current cycle may produce greater scarcity and
pricing power than demand growth alone would suggest.

So far, this dynamic has been most visible in industrial metals, but the same forces could
extend into agriculture. Reshoring changes where food and inputs are produced and
processed, while inventory rebuilding can lift demand for fertilizer, fuel, machinery and
storage. Agriculture has not yet experienced the same earnings and pricing impact as
metals, but supply-chain resilience could make it a broader beneficiary over time.
Cyclical forces still matter: The structural thesis does not eliminate near-term

volatility. Energy remains a key transmission mechanism for inflation because oil and
natural gas underpin transportation, manufacturing and industrial production. Geopolitical
disruptions can cascade through supply chains, raising costs and moving both commodity
futures and spot prices. Currency dynamics matter as well. A stronger U.S. dollar can
restrain commodity prices by increasing costs for non-U.S. buyers, while renewed dollar
weakness could support emerging-market demand and reprice dollar-denominated

assets.
A stabilization of geopolitical tensions could create a more constructive cyclical backdrop
through lower energy volatility, improved business confidence and stronger capital
investment. Conversely, renewed conflict can produce sharp and uneven moves across
the complex. These crosscurrents reinforce the value of treating commodities as a
diversified allocation rather than a single directional trade.
Gold, implementation and the portfolio case: Gold is fundamentally different from

most industrial commodities, but it deserves attention. Its traditional relationships with
interest rates and the dollar have been less reliable lately. Even so, sustained central bank
and exchange-traded fund buying points to durable long-term demand despite bouts of
volatility such as the selloff earlier this year. Gold also tended to move inversely with oil
during the height of the Iran conflict, illustrating how different commodities can respond

differently to the same geopolitical shock.
Investors can access the theme through commodity futures or producer Equities. Direct
commodity exposure is generally the clearer vehicle for inflation protection, scarcity, and
geopolitical supply shocks. Producer equities can offer greater upside when commodity
prices rise faster than operating costs because earnings and margins expand, but that
leverage is asymmetric. Company-specific execution, financing, cost and valuation risks

can still cause Equities to underperform the underlying commodity. A blended approach
may capture both physical scarcity and corporate operating leverage.
Ultimately, commodities remain sensitive to energy markets, currencies, growth, and
geopolitics, but their portfolio role is expanding. They can provide inflation protection and
diversification while offering exposure to AI infrastructure, electrification, energy security,
defense modernization, reshoring and, potentially, agricultural supply-chain investment.

The strongest beneficiaries of the AI trade may therefore extend well beyond technology
companies to the materials and power systems that make deployment possible.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Rising Global Interest Rates and International Markets
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Strong earnings growth has so far delivered a fourth consecutive year of double-digit
gains for global Equity indexes in 2026, propelling them to new all-time highs. But a
growing risk throughout the year has been the increase in government bond yields, which

have also broken out to multi-decade highs across a number of the major markets. The
U.S. Treasury will this week begin its expanded Treasury securities buyback program
announced in mid-August in response to rising rates. But 10-year yields across all
the major G10 countries have moved higher, in many cases by as much as or more
than in the U.S. market. Against a backdrop of above-trend growth in nominal gross
domestic product (GDP), high energy prices and elevated inflation, investors appear to

be reassessing monetary policy expectations, particularly in Europe where most of the
2026 increase in 10-year yields has been driven by inflation breakevens (Exhibit 1). Fixed
Income markets are consequently looking for policy rate hikes from the European Central
Bank later this week and potentially from the Bank of Japan later next month.
The rise in global interest rates has also come alongside an expansion in fiscal policy
across the major markets. In Europe, 2026 and 2027 are expected to be the peak

spending years for defense and infrastructure expenditure plans unveiled last year. The
so-called Readiness 2030 package allocates €800 billion to increased European Union
defense spending as it looks to move toward its 5% of GDP target by 2035. And at the
same time, the German government relaxation of its national debt brake has directed
€500 billion toward funding for investment in clean energy, transportation and digital
infrastructure. Similarly in Japan, the government supermajority is looking to enact a

new stimulus aimed at raising investment levels across 17 strategic sectors such as
semiconductors, defense and shipbuilding, in addition to a planned two-year cut to the
consumption tax on food from April 2027. And in the UK, the new central government
leadership is also likely to announce new spending measures when its first national
budget is released next month. To the extent that increased public investment spending
can lift productivity and support nominal GDP growth in Europe and Japan, the rise in

yields need not undermine economic growth or derail the equity market advance. But
higher rates, especially in response to supply-driven energy price inflation, nonetheless
remain a risk for developed market equity returns going into the end of the year.
Exhibit 1: Global Bond Yields in Major Markets have Risen by as Much or More
than U.S. Yields in 2026.

For many emerging markets the picture looks quite different. In contrast with the U.S.,
Japan and Europe, inflation rates are generally well below central bank targets outside
a limited number of resource exporters in Latin America and the booming AI-linked
economies of Korea and Taiwan (Exhibit 2). As a result, policy rate expectations over

the coming quarters are stable or lower for several emerging markets (including China,
Malaysia, Thailand, Poland, Brazil and Mexico) according to Bloomberg consensus forecasts.
Investment Implications
Ongoing strength in economic growth
and corporate earnings should support
global equity markets even in the face
of rising rates. For Emerging Markets
(EM) Equities in particular, contained
inflation, flat to lower local policy rate
expectations and stronger fundamentals

should limit the impact. And we
continue to favor EM Equities over
International Developed Markets, where
inflation levels are generally still above
target, central banks are expected
to raise rates and investors will be
watching for fiscal sustainability as
governments implement expansionary
budget plans.

Historically, rising U.S. interest rates have been a major headwind for emerging markets.
But looking ahead in the current cycle, we still expect the impact of potential Federal
Reserve (Fed) rate hikes to be lower than in the past. Over recent years, the association
between tighter Fed policy and weaker EM returns appears to have diminished. EM
Equities for example were resilient in the face of aggressive U.S. rate increases in 2022
and 2023, broadly keeping pace with the rest of the world. This is in sharp contrast to

prior cycles in which tighter Fed policy has resulted in capital flight, deleveraging and
falling asset values across the emerging world. Particularly during the crisis periods of
the 1980s and 1990s—but also more recently during the 2015 taper tantrum—U.S.
monetary tightening has tended to attract capital away from riskier emerging countries
(particularly those with large current account deficits), reducing support for local asset
prices and exchange rates.

Associated strength in the U.S. dollar has typically been an additional hurdle. For those
emerging economies with fixed exchange rates, a loss of export competitiveness has
further undermined current account positions, forcing central banks to run down their
foreign exchange reserves. And where exchange rates are floating, foreign currency
debts have risen in local currency terms, resulting in widespread defaults, debt
downgrades, recessions and additional exchange rate depreciation.

But starting in the years that followed the 1997-1998 Asian financial crisis and
continuing into the current cycle, the fundamentals across much of the EM universe have
improved. Fixed exchange rates have been abandoned. EM foreign currency reserves have
roughly doubled as a share of GDP . The share of EM debt denominated in U.S. dollars
has fallen significantly, led by the private sector across households and corporates. And
for most countries, current account deficits have narrowed or disappeared entirely. As a

result, emerging markets have become much more insulated from the direction of U.S.
interest rates on a structural basis, and this has only been reinforced by the AI-driven
revaluation of key Asian markets in 2026. Greater concentration in Korea and Taiwan has
increased the EM weighting in the surplus Asia-Pacific region to 83% as at the end of
August—up from 81% at the start of the year and 71% a decade ago.
Above-trend growth in nominal GDP and ongoing strength in corporate earnings should

support global equity markets even in the face of rising interest rates. But for emerging
Equities in particular, contained inflation relative to central bank targets, flat to lower
local policy rate expectations and stronger economic fundamentals should limit the
impact from higher global rates. We continue to favor EM Equities over International
Developed Markets, where inflation levels are generally still above target, central banks
are expected to raise interest rates and investors will be watching for fiscal sustainability

as governments implement expansionary budget plans.

### Page 6

6 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The One-Offs Inflating Q2 Earnings
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Headline Q2 earnings numbers look almost too good to be true. And in a sense, they are.
With nearly all of S&P 500 companies’ results reported, EPS growth is tracking around
50% year-over-year (YoY)—not just an acceleration from Q1 but a second consecutive
quarter above 20%. Chiefly skewing the headline number are massive mark-to-market

gains on private investments for a handful of mega cap technology companies like
Alphabet and Amazon that booked outsized non-operating gains tied to their stakes in
Anthropic, while Microsoft benefited from its OpenAI investment. Strip those out, and
adjusted EPS growth notches down to 31%. The point being, after being sliced and
diced, 30%+ is well above any long-run historical earnings growth rate.
The one-off nature of earnings strength wasn’t limited to investment gains. A second,

less-discussed boost was tariff refunds. As Exhibit 3 illustrates, revenues from tariffs
steadily climbed from early 2025 through mid-2026 until the International Emergency
Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariff ruling which initiated the refund process. As
refunds were disbursed, customs receipts turned net negative in June and July, with
refund payments exceeding total tariff collections. Customs and Border Protection cash
withdrawals of more than $100 billion in refunds have now been paid—60% of the $165

billion in total eligible claims.
The recipients: the “importers of record,” or the dozens of S&P 500 companies that
have disclosed billions in refunds over the past two quarters; Apple alone received $2.19
billion, adding $0.11 to per-share earnings to their quarter. Other disclosed refunds
include Nike with $986 million, Amazon with $640 million, and General Motors refunded
$500 million (although accounting treatment and use of proceeds has varied).

The boost of investment gains and/or refunds may not be repeatable in future quarters.
Perhaps more telling is that the median company’s revenue grew roughly 15% YoY, the
strongest gain in years, corroborating that there is operating momentum rather than
just an accounting mirage for Q2 earnings. Margins, too, as a percent of GDP are at
their highest back to 1950, hovering north of 22%, see Exhibit 4. That this is occurring
amid rising rates and at a time of geopolitical uncertainty/disruption, rather than a post-

recessionary recovery, makes the results that much more impressive.
The Path Forward: Questions remain about the sustainability of these elevated growth
rates, although consensus expects EPS growth to stay above 20% in both Q3 and Q4.
If achieved, every quarter this year would see 20%-plus EPS growth. On the back of
brighter outlooks from Corporate America in recent weeks, full-year earnings growth
will decelerate into the mid-teens in 2027, but that moderation comes off a high base

of 2026. For now, the breadth of earnings growth is the encouraging story as 10 of
the 11 sectors reported growth (Energy and Communication Services leading the way,
Healthcare the only detractor). That’s a reflection of strong core businesses growing
revenues into 2027’s tougher YoY comparisons.
Investment Implications
Underlying earnings-per-share (EPS)
growth of around 30%, stripped
of investment gains, is historically
exceptional. It remains that robust

corporate earnings and positive
revisions have helped Equities absorb
concerns around the recent rise
in yields.

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 214: De-reservification, Not De-dollarization (2026-09-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/De-reservification, Not De-dollarization (2026-09-15).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-09-15
Pages: 17
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
De-reservification, Not De-dollarization
The world isn’t moving away from the dollar. It is shifting its dollars from traditional
reserves to state banks, pension funds, and other quasi-sovereign investors.
(Bernardo Montoya/Courtesy Reuters)
PUBLISHED
September 15, 2026 8:37 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
This is a joint blog with Alex Etra, Senior Macro
Strategist and head of Flow of Funds at Vanda
Research.
The IMF’s data on the currency composition of
foreign exchange (FX) reserves is scrutinized carefully
for any signs that the world is shifting way from the
dollar.
Yet it doesn’t really matter that much if the dollar’s
share of reserves has shifted from 56.5 percent to 57
percent. The ink-to-impact ratio of the quarterly

reporting on the latest COFER data is all off.
Neither the stock of global reserves nor the stock of
dollar reserves has changed much in the last ten years.
The action is elsewhere.

### Page 3

3
China’s state banks (per the Bank of International
Settlements) have almost as many foreign assets as the
central bank (PBOC).
Japan’s Government Pension Investment Fund
(GPIF) has almost as many foreign assets ($986
billion) as the government has FX reserves ($1.1
trillion at the end of August). Japan’s FX reserves are
on the books of the Ministry of Finance and the
GPIF is overseen by the Ministry of Health, Welfare
and Labor—so these are almost all assets of the

Government of Japan or the broader public sector,
not its central bank.
Korea’s National Pension Service has more foreign
assets than the Bank of Korea has FX reserves.
A different dynamic is in play in Taiwan, as the
government doesn’t run a big retirement fund. But in

### Page 4

4
a world where the hedge ratio of the life insurers has
been (and still is) used as a policy tool, the shift in
Taiwan’s foreign asset accumulation away from the
Central Bank of China (CBC) and over to
institutions whose activities in the foreign currency
market it directly influences broadly fits into the same
theme. So too does the PBOC’s use of the
“macroprudential adjustment factor” effectively adjust
banks’ net foreign assets in much the same way that

CBC regulations adjust lifer hedge ratios.
In the last year, the lifers’ hedge ratios—and their
direct hedges with the central bank—have been one
of the key tools the CBC has used to manage the
Taiwan dollar (and guard the lifers’ solvency).
Asia’s big surplus economies are in a sense just
catching up with the world’s oil exporters. Norway
put its oil surplus into a sovereign wealth fund from
the start. Kuwait, Qatar, and Abu Dhabi (the most
oil-rich emirate) all also have large sovereign funds.

And the world’s biggest oil exporter has joined them:
The Public Investment Fund has almost as many
foreign assets as the Saudi Central Bank (SAMA).*
What’s more, the available evidence suggests that the
dollar share of these pools is in line with or higher
than the dollar share of global FX reserves (it depends
on the institution and jurisdiction).

### Page 5

5
In other words, by looking at the (easily available)
data on the world’s static holdings of formal FX
reserve assets, scholars and analysts miss most of the
growth in the world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign
foreign assets.
Consider China.
No serious analyst now disputes that China’s state
banks—including the policy banks (the China
Development Bank, China Exim)—hold several
trillion in foreign assets.
China reports $3.3 trillion in gross foreign assets to

the BIS (the net position, counting foreign bank
claims on the entire Chinese economy not just the
banks, is $2.5 trillion). That maps to the BOP data,
which shows almost $4 trillion in gross outflows
through the banking system (technically, the sum of
gross outflows in “other” plus the $500 billion in
foreign currency bonds held by the state commercial
banks)

### Page 6

6
Since 2010 (and even more so after 2014) the net
outflow through the state banks has exceeded reserve
accumulation.
The broad contours of this story are confirmed by the
balance sheet data reported by state commercial
banks in their 2025 annual reports, which showed

### Page 7

7
that the top five banks held a combined $2.5 trillion
in foreign currency assets (mostly held abroad).
The Chinese haven’t disclosed the foreign assets of
the two policy banks (with at this stage the complicity
of the IMF, which has neither analyzed the role of
SAFE policy bank financing, nor highlighted the
glaring gap in China’s own reporting). But the work
of AidData points to nearly $1 trillion in foreign
assets, with a hefty dollar share.
The available data sources all suggest that the bulk of

banks—puts the dollar share of their offshore foreign
currency assets at around 70 percent. That is well
above the last disclosed dollar share of China’s formal
reserves (55 percent in 2019). The banks’ annual

### Page 8

8
reports suggest that about 70 percent of the banks’
assets are in dollars and Hong Kong dollars (55
percent USD/15 percent HKD)—a bit below the
dollar and Hong Kong dollar share of their liabilities
(swaps out of the HKD fund a decent share of the
banks euro assets).
Put simply, SAFE’s static dollar holdings aren’t the
important story.
The real story is the rapid growth of the state banks
dollar holdings.
Japan holds a high (though undisclosed) dollar share

in its FX reserves, and those reserves are primarily
invested in Treasuries—so Japan’s MoF is now clearly
the largest contributor to the U.S. data on foreign
official holdings of Treasuries. (SAFE has shifted its
funds out of U.S. custodians, and thus increasingly
appears in the data as a “private” holder in a European
custodial center)
Japan’s formal reserves haven’t changed much in the
last ten years; periodic sales to limit the yen’s weakness
have offset most of the MoF’s accumulated interest

income. Foreign currency reserves were $1.2 trillion
in 2014 and $1.16 trillion at the end of Q1. They
dipped to about $1 trillion at the end of August after
sales of close to $100 billion at the end of July/start of
August.

### Page 9

9
What has grown is the foreign portfolio of the GPIF.
it was around $400 billion in 2014, ~$1 trillion in Q1
of 2026.
GPIF’s foreign bonds aren’t held exclusively in dollars,
but roughly 52 percent of its foreign bonds (26
percent of all bonds) are USD-denominated.
But the GPIF, like most international investors, holds
a large share of its equity portfolio in the U.S. (~$165
billion, 65 percent of the foreign equity portfolio).
Korea is a more extreme version of Japan.

The Bank of Korea’s FX reserves have been roughly
$375-450 billion over the last ten years or so. Around
two-thirds of those are now in dollars (as of the end of
2025).

### Page 10

10
From 2012 to 2025, the foreign assets at the NPS
have grown from ~$80 billion (around 21 percent of
total assets) to over $660 billion (55 percent of total
assets)—and something like 70 percent of those are in
dollars. 76 percent of the fund’s equities are in dollars,
and 66 percent of its debt. And while a lower share of
its “alternative” portfolio is in North America, most
alternative mandates are awarded in dollars.

### Page 11

11
In other words, the majority of the dollar assets held
by Korea’s government (the NPS reports to the
Ministry of Health and Welfare, and the Minister of
Health chairs its asset allocation committee) aren’t
held by the central bank and aren’t in the IMF’s
COFER data set.
Taiwan is an unusual case, as the life insurers aren’t
technically sovereign investors. But with their
regulator now explicitly encouraging the them to hold
an unhedged foreign currency book and hence they

now rely on the state for their solvency, they have a bit
of an official character (a significant appreciation of
the TWD would blow through their volatility reserves
and leave them with fewer assets than liabilities, as
their foreign assets are largely held against TWD
policies). The CBC certainly now uses the lifers desire
to unwind existing hedges to help manage the Taiwan

### Page 12

12
dollar. And they have an exceptionally high dollar
share in their foreign assets—over 95 percent.
The Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF) is another
example.
It keeps roughly $200 billion of its $250 billion in
foreign investments in dollars—well above the U.S.
share of a standard global stock index. Almost all of
the PIF’s $30 billion in external debt is also in dollars.
And the PIF now has only slightly fewer foreign
assets than the Saudi Central Bank has reserves.**

The broad story is thus pretty clear: the growth in the
world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign assets is not
coming through an increase in FX reserve holdings
managed by the world’s central banks.

### Page 13

13
And, in the critical case of China, the dollar share of
China’s quasi-sovereign foreign assets is likely higher
than the dollar share of their formal FX reserves.
Adam Tooze has noted that inflows into the U.S. are
now coming from investors looking for a profit, not
investors looking for safety. What he calls the “profit
dollar” manifests itself in a number of different ways.
More inflows into corporate bonds and equities for
one. Less official demand for Treasuries for another.

More opacity too.
Those same trends are at play among sovereign and
quasi-sovereign investors.
So don’t obsess about the dollar’s chare in formal FX
reserves. Do recognize that the dollar’s “reserve
currency role” isn’t the source of any significant new
inflows into the dollar.

### Page 14

14
The dollar’s dominant role in the international
monetary system depends on much more than the
size and composition of central bank FX reserves. It is
as much, perhaps more so (given that FX reserve
managers are themselves ultimately liability matchers)
a function of the portfolio choice of a set of
private/semi-private/quasi-sovereign investors that
are much more difficult to observe. Reserve currency
status is not only about FX reserves in a strict sense.

That’s been true for some time. Most of the current
flow into U.S. from the “official” sector is coming
from investors who are not classic reserve managers.
Those flows remain heavily tilted toward the dollar, at
least for now.***
And any real de-dollarization would likely occur first
among these investors.
Put differently, the dollar’s global role is increasingly
as a source of returns, not a source of safety. The
foreign bid, private and public, is for risk, not for

Treasuries. That doesn’t help Scott Bessent much
right now, but it has helped keep valuations in the
stock market extended. And the global debate on the
dollar’s role lags the evolution in the dollar’s role, and
the risks associated with that new role.
It is currently fashionable in some circles to point to
the diminution of the “convenience yield” on

### Page 15

15
Treasuries even while the U.S. dollar continues to
enjoy such a privilege. But the shift in the global
investor base, and their apparent portfolio preference
for U.S. risk assets in lieu of Treasuries, may help
explain the divergence between the convenience yield
on UST and the convenience yield on USD.
And of course, if the negative convenience yield
implies that Treasuries are trading at a historical
discount, the persistence of the latter—and its

concentration in risk assets—suggests that the “profit
dollar” itself is likely trading at a historical premium.
That apparent premium on U.S. risk assets deserves as
much (or more) scrutiny as the currency composition
of central bank FX reserves.
* The PIF and Saudi Aramco also now have a certain
amount of debt.
** Reserves (now mostly in Treasuries, so should
appear in the TIC data, unlike at some times in the
past.

### Page 16

16

Council on Foreign Relations is an independent,
nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and
publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters
of policy.

share is below the likely dollar share of Japan’s MoF.
